Large deviation principle for a class of stochastic partial differential equations with fully local monotone coefficients perturbed by Lévy noise 1,2 Department of Mathematics, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee-IIT Roorkee, Haridwar Highway, Roorkee, Uttarakhand 247667, INDIA. e-mail: Manil T. Mohan: maniltmohan@ma.iitr.ac.in, maniltmohan@gmail.com. e-mail: Ankit Kumar: akumar14@mt.iitr.ac.in. * Corresponding author. Key words: Stochastic partial differential equations, locally monotne, pseudo-monotone, Lévy noise, Wentzell-Freidlin large deviation, weak convergence. Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): Primary 60H15, 60F10; Secondary 35R60, 35Q35, 37L55.
Ankit Kumar 1 and Manil T. Mohan 2*

Abstract
. The asymptotic analysis of a class of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) with fully locally monotone coefficients covering a large variety of physical systems, a wide class of quasilinear SPDEs and a good number of fluid dynamic models is carried out in this work. The aim of this work is to develop the large deviation theory for small Gaussian as well as Poisson noise perturbations of the above class of SPDEs. We establish a Wentzell-Freidlin type large deviation principle for the strong solutions to such SPDEs perturbed by Lévy noise in a suitable Polish space using a variational representation (based on a weak convergence approach) for nonnegative functionals of general Poisson random measures and Brownian motions. The well-posedness of an associated deterministic control problem is established by exploiting pseudo-monotonicity arguments and the stochastic counterpart is obtained by an application of Girsanov’s theorem.



1.
Introduction

Let us denote a separable Hilbert space and a reflexive Banach space by
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{V}
, respectively, and let the embedding
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
be continuous and dense. Denote the dual space of
\mathbb{V}
by
\mathbb{V}^{*}
and let
\mathbb{H}^{*}\cong\mathbb{H}
. The norms of
\mathbb{H},\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{V}^{*}
are denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}
, respectively, and we have a Gelfand triplet
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{V}^{*}
. We represent
(\cdot,\cdot),
the inner product in the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,
the duality pairing between
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{V}^{*}
. Also,
\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
, whenever
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}
. The space of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\mathbb{H}
to itself is denoted by
\mathrm{L}_{2}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
(for convenience, we use
\mathrm{L}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
) with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}
and the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space equipped with an increasing family of sub-sigma fields
\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of
\mathscr{F}
satisfying:

(i)

\mathscr{F}_{0}
contains all elements
A\in\mathscr{F}
with
\mathbb{P}(A)=0
.

(ii)

\mathscr{F}_{t}=\mathscr{F}_{t+}=\cap_{s>t}\mathscr{F}_{s}
, for
t\geq 0
.

In this work, we establish a Wentzell-Freidlin type large deviation principle (LDP) for the following class of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) with fully locally monotone coefficients in the Gelfand triplet
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{V}^{*}
driven by Lévy noise:

(1.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t)&=\mathrm{A}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathrm{B}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{W}(t)+\epsilon\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t-),z)\widetilde{N}^{\epsilon^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z),\\
\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H},\end{aligned}}}\right.

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where the mappings

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{*},\;\mathrm{B}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathrm{L}_{2}(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H}),\text{ and }\gamma:[0,T]\times\mathbb{V}\times\mathrm{Z}\to\mathbb{H},

are measurable,
\mathrm{Z}
is a locally compact Polish space,
\mathrm{W}(\cdot)
is an
\mathbb{H}
-valued cylindrical Wiener process on the filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
and
\widetilde{N}^{\epsilon^{-1}}
is a Poisson random measure on
[0,T]\times\mathrm{Z}
with a
\sigma
-finite intensity measure
\epsilon^{-1}\lambda_{T}\otimes\nu
,
\lambda_{T}
is the Lebesgue measure on
[0,T]
and
\nu
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathrm{Z}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{N}^{\epsilon^{-1}}([0,t]\times O)=N^{\epsilon^{-1}}([0,t]\times O)-\epsilon^{-1}t\nu(O)
, for all
O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z})
with
\nu(O)<\infty
, is the compensated Poisson random measure. The well-posedness of the problem (
1.1
) with
\epsilon=0
is discussed in
[
45
]
and the global solvability of the system (
1.1
) perturbed by Gaussian and Lévy noises are considered in
[
65
,
41
]
, respectively.

1.1.
Hypothesis and solvability results

Let us first discuss the assumptions satisfied by the mappings and recall the solvability results for the system (
1.1
) from
[
41
]
.
The mappings
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
and
\gamma
satisfy the following Hypothesis:

Hypothesis 1.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{+})
and
\beta\in(1,\infty)
.

(H.1)

(Hemicontinuity)
. The map
\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x}+\lambda\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{z}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
is continuous for any
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{V}
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

(H.2)

(Local monotonicity)
. There exist non negative constants
\zeta
and
C
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle 2\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\rangle+

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{y})\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}^{2}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x},z)-\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{y},z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)

(1.2)

\displaystyle\leq\big[\mathfrak{a}(t)+\rho(\boldsymbol{x})+\eta(\boldsymbol{y})\big]\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},

\displaystyle|\rho(\boldsymbol{x})|+|\eta(\boldsymbol{x})|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta})(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\zeta}),

where
\rho
and
\eta
are two measurable functions from
\mathbb{V}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

(H.3)

(Coercivity)
. There exists a positive constant
C
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\leq\mathfrak{a}(t)(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2})-L_{\mathrm{A}}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}.

(H.4)

(Growth)
. There exist non-negative constants
\alpha
and
C
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\leq(\mathfrak{a}(t)+C\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta})(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}).

(H.5)

For any sequence
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{V}
with
\|\boldsymbol{x}_{n}-\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
, we have

(1.5)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})-\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}\to 0,\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T].

Moreover, there exists
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq\mathfrak{b}(t)(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}).

(H.6)

The jump noise coefficient
\gamma(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies:

(1)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\zeta(t,\cdot,z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t\bigg]<\infty.

(2)

For any sequence
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{V}
with
\|\boldsymbol{x}_{n}-\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
, we have

(1.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x}_{n},z)-\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x},z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)\to 0,\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T].

(3)

There exist functions
\mathfrak{h}_{p}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
, a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x},z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{p}\lambda(\mathrm{d}z)\leq\mathfrak{h}_{p}(t)(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{p}),\text{ for }p\in[2,\infty).

Let us now introduce the concept of solution and its uniqueness to the system (
1.1
). We denote
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
for the space of all càdlàg functions (right continuous with left limits) from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{H}
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
be a given stochastic basis and the initial data
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
. Then (
1.1
) has a
probabilistically strong solution
if and only if there exists a progressively measurable process
\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., with paths

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s., for }\beta\in(1,\infty),

and the following equality holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., in
\mathbb{V}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{A}(s,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{B}(s,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(s))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{W}(s)

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(s,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(s-),z)\widetilde{N}^{\epsilon^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z),\;t\in[0,T].

Definition 1.3
.

We say that the system (
1.1
) admits a
pathwise unique solution
if any two
\mathbb{H}
-valued solutions
\mathbf{Y}_{1}(\cdot)
and
\mathbf{Y}_{2}(\cdot)
defined on the same probability space with respect to the same Poisson random measure and Wiener process starting from the same initial data
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
coincide almost surely.

With a minor modification in Theorem 2.11,
[
41
]
, one can establish the following result on the existence of a unique pathwise strong solution to the system (
1.1
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
1.1
(H.1)-(H.6) hold and the embedding
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
is compact. Then, for any initial data
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
, there exists a
probabilistically strong (analytically weak) solution
to the system (
1.1
). Furthermore, for any
p\geq 2
, the following estimate holds:

(1.9)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\mathbf{Y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{p}\bigg]+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbf{Y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t\bigg]^{\frac{p}{2}}<\infty.

1.2.
Literature survey

The existence and uniqueness of solutions for the deterministic problem corresponding to the system (
1.1
) has been established in
[
45
]
, and the stochastic counterpart has been discussed in the works
[
65
,
41
]
, where the authors considered the system (
1.1
) perturbed by multiplicative Gaussian and Lévy noises, respectively. The global solvability of time fractional SPDEs with fully locally monotone coefficients driven by additive Gaussian noise is studied in
[
49
,
50
]
. Several authors proved the well-posedness of SPDEs with locally monotone coefficients, cf.
[
15
,
33
,
43
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
64
]
, etc. Well-posedness of a class of SPDEs driven by Lévy noise with generalized coercivity condition, and with locally monotone coefficients is obtained in
[
15
]
, and with monotone coefficients is established in
[
39
]
. Solvability results for different types of SPDEs driven by Lévy noise have been established in
[
13
,
16
,
31
,
57
,
59
,
60
,
72
,
76
]
, etc., and references therein.

The theory of large deviations, which provides asymptotic estimates for probabilities of rare events is one of the most classical areas and important research topics in probability theory and has rightly received attention after the framework and applications provided by Stroock and Varadhan in
[
70
,
74
]
, respectively, and extended its applications to different areas (cf.
[
28
,
29
,
70
]
, etc). The authors in
[
23
,
37
,
68
]
, etc., proved Wentzell-Freidlin type LDP for different classes of SPDEs. The weak convergence approach (cf.
[
20
]
) has been explored in the works
[
22
,
44
,
26
]
to obtain large deviations principle for the small noise limit of the systems of stochastic reaction-diffusion equations with globally Lipschitz but unbounded coefficients, stochastic evolution equations with general monotone drift and for a class of stochastic 2D hydrodynamical type systems, respectively, driven by multiplicative Gaussian noise.

From the last few decades, several authors have established the LDP for different type of SPDEs perturbed by pure jump and Lévy noises. In the works
[
1
,
2
]
, the authors obtained the LDP for the solutions of stochastic differential equations (SDEs) perturbed by Poisson random measures. The authors in
[
18
,
19
]
used the results due to Varadhan and Bryc
[
28
]
, and extended the earlier results of Budhiraja and Dupis
[
17
]
to establish the LDP for SDEs with Poisson noises by first proving the Laplace principle in Polish spaces using the weak convergence approach. The large deviation theory for small Poisson noise perturbations of a general class of deterministic infinite dimensional models is developed in
[
20
]
. The authors in
[
66
]
established an LDP for a class of SPDEs perturbed by an addditive jump noise. The LDP for the solutions of an abstract stochastic evolution equations driven by small Lévy noise is obtained in
[
71
]
. Using the methodology developed in
[
1
,
2
]
, the authors in
[
75
]
proved the LDP for 2D Navier-Stokes equations perturbed by additive Lévy noise. Using the results established in
[
20
]
, the LDP for two dimensional Navier-Stokes equations governed by Lévy noise is obtained in
[
16
,
79
]
, and for a class of SPDEs with locally monotone coefficients driven by pure jump noise is established in
[
76
]
. Using the same weak convergence approach (cf.
[
20
]
), several authors established the LDP for many physically relevant models perturbed by Lévy noise, cf.
[
9
,
31
,
53
,
58
]
, etc.

1.3.
Novelties, difficulties and approaches

The main goal of this work is to develop the large deviation theory for small Gaussian as well as Poisson noise perturbations of a class of SPDEs which covers a wide class of physical models. Very recently, the authors in
[
65
]
established well-posedness results for a class of SPDEs with fully local monotone coefficients perturbed by multiplicative Gaussian noise under various assumptions on the noise coefficient. Then, the authors in
[
41
]
extended the theory developed in
[
65
]
from multiplicative Gaussian to Lévy noise and showed the existence of a uniue probabilistically strong solution in the space
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
. Even though SPDEs with locally monotone coefficients are well-studied (cf.
[
15
,
33
,
34
,
38
,
42
,
43
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
64
,
76
]
, etc., and references therein), the systems having fully local monotone coefficients are not much explored in the literature, since the well-posedness results were not known previously.

We establish a Wentzell-Freidlin type large deviation principle for the strong solutions to the system (
1.1
) in the state space
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
using a variational representation (based on a weak convergence approach). We exploit the weak convergence method developed by Budhiraja et.al. in
[
17
,
18
,
20
]
, etc., where the authors established the LDP using a variational representation for nonnegative functionals of general Poisson random measures and Brownian motions (see Condition
2.1
and Theorem
3.6
below). In order to establish the LDP for the system (
1.1
), we follow the works
[
76
,
79
]
, where the authors established the LDP, for a class of SPDEs with locally monotone coefficients driven by pure jump noise, 2D stochastic Navier-Stokes equations perturbed by Lévy noise, respectively. The well-posedness of the associated deterministic control problem (see (
4.1
) below) is established by exploiting pseudo-monotonicity arguments and the stochastic control problem (see (
5.1
) below) is obtained by an application of Girsanov’s theorem. Moreover, Skorokhod’s representation theorem is used to verify Condition
2.1
(2), which is a law of large numbers result for stochastic controlled systems with small noise. The LDP for the system (
1.1
) is established under some additional assumptions on the noise coefficients (see Hypothesis
3.4
below), while the same problem is open under Hypothesis
1.1
.

1.4.
Organization of the paper

This article is organized as follows: In Section
2
, we provide some basic results related to pseudo-monotone operators (Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
) and a general criteria of LDP along with a sufficient condition (see Condition
2.1
) for LDP. In Section
3
, we recall some useful results from
[
76
,
79
]
which help us to obtain LDP for the SPDE under our consideration. We need some additional assumptions on the noise coefficients to obtain LDP for the system (
1.1
) and we collect them in Hypothesis
3.4
. We state our main result as Theorem
3.6
, whose proof rely on verifying Condition
2.1
. In order to verify Condition
2.1
(1), we first establish the existence and uniqueness result of the deterministic control system (
4.1
) using a Faedo-Galerkin approximation technique and pseudo-monotonicity property of the nonlinear operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
(see Theorem
4.1
). With the aid of Theorem
4.1
, Condition
2.1
(1) is verified in Proposition
4.2
. Section
5
is devoted for the verification of Condition
2.1
(2), which is the most difficult part of the work. Firstly, we consider the stochastic control problem (
5.1
) and discuss the existence and uniqueness of its pathwise strong solution by using Girsanov’s theorem (Lemma
5.3
). In order to prove the tightness of the laws of solutions of (
5.1
), we first obtain the uniform bounds (independent of
\epsilon
) in Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
. The tightness of the laws of solutions in the space
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{V}^{*})
with the Skorokhod topology is established in Lemma
5.7
. Convergence of the processes and proper identification of limits is carried forward in Lemmas
5.8
,
5.9
,
5.11
and
5.13
. The solvability results of the limiting process is discussed in Proposition
5.6
. We wind up the article with the proof of Theorem
5.14
verifying Condition
2.1
(2) and the main ingredient of the proof is celebrated Skorokhod’s representation theorem. Some important applications of the model described in this work is provided in Subsection
5.4
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we provide results related to pseudo-monotone operators (see
[
45
,
65
]
) and a general criteria for LDP given in
[
20
]
. For LDP, we follow the framework and notations from
[
19
,
20
,
76
,
79
]
, etc.

2.1.
Monotonicity

In this subsection, we recall some results related to the operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Definition 2.1
.

An operator
\mathrm{A}:\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{*}
is said to be
pseudo-monotone
if the following condition holds:
for any sequence
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
with the weak limit
\boldsymbol{v}
in
\mathbb{V}
and

(2.1)

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to 0}\langle\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{m}),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}-\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\geq 0,

imply that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\limsup_{m\to\infty}\langle\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{m}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\leq\langle\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{u}\rangle,\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{V}.

Let us now recall some useful results from
[
45
,
65
]
, which help us to obtain the well-posedness of SPDEs under our consideration.

Proposition 2.2

(Proposition 2.1,
[
45
]
)
.

Suppose that the operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot)
is pseudo-monotone and
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\boldsymbol{v}
weakly in
\mathbb{V},
then

(2.3)

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to\infty}\langle\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{m}),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}-\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\leq 0.

The next result plays a crucial role in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. The lemma says that
\boldsymbol{v}\mapsto\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{v})
is pseudo-monotone from
\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
to
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*})
, for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
. The detailed proof can be obtained from
[
30
,
45
,
69
]
, etc.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
\beta\in(1,\infty)
, under Hypothesis
1.1
, assume that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{m}

\displaystyle\xrightharpoonup{}\boldsymbol{v}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot))

\displaystyle\xrightharpoonup{}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*}),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t,

then for any
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t\geq\limsup_{m\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

Using Hypothesis
1.1
(H.3), (H.4) and Young’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\rangle-\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)),\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{a}(\cdot)\big(1+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)-L_{A}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}+\|\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot))\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle\leq-L_{\mathrm{A}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}+\mathfrak{a}(\cdot)\big(1+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\big[\mathfrak{a}(\cdot)+C\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}\big]^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}\big[1+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}\big]^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{V}}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{L_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}+\mathfrak{a}(\cdot)\big(2+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)+C\|\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha(\beta-1)}\|\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta},

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. For simplification, we write

\displaystyle g_{m}(t)

\displaystyle:=\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\rangle,

\displaystyle F_{m}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{a}(t)\big(2+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)+C\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha(\beta-1)}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}.

Thus, (
2.1
) reduces to

\displaystyle g_{m}(t)\leq-\frac{L_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}+F_{m}(t),

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. The rest of the proof follows in a similar lines as in the proof of Lemma 2.5,
[
45
]
by establishing

(1)

For a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle\limsup_{m\to\infty}g_{m}(t)\leq 0;

(2)

there exists a subsequence
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(2.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}g_{m_{i}}(t)=0,\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t\in[0,T].

Then exploiting the pseudo-monotone property of the operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
(see Remark 2.2,
[
65
]
) and Fatou’s lemma, one can complete the proof.
∎

Let us now rephrase Lemma
2.3
in the random case. That is, one can show that the operator
\mathbf{Y}(\cdot)\mapsto\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\mathbf{Y}(\cdot))
is pseudo-monotone from
\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathbb{V})
to
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathbb{V}^{*})
, for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
.

Lemma 2.4

(Lemma 2.16,
[
65
]
)
.

If

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\xrightharpoonup{}\mathbf{Y},\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathbb{V}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(\cdot))

\displaystyle\xrightharpoonup{}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot),\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathbb{V}^{*}),

(2.9)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t)),\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t),\mathbf{Y}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t\bigg],

then
\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)=\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(\cdot)),\;\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.

2.2.
Large deviation principle

Let
\{\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a family of random variables defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
and taking values in a Polish space
\mathcal{E}
. The LDP is concerned with the exponential decay of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\in O)
as
\epsilon\to 0
, for an event
O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
. The exponential decay rate of such probabilities is generally expressed in terms of a ‘rate function’
\mathrm{I}
defined as follows:

Definition 2.5

(Rate function)
.

A function
\mathrm{I}:\mathcal{E}\to[0,\infty]
is called a
rate function
on
\mathcal{E}
, if for any
M<\infty
, the level set
\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathcal{E}:\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{x})\leq M\}
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{E}
. For
O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
, we define
\mathrm{I}(O)=\inf\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in O}\mathrm{I}(\boldsymbol{x})
.

Definition 2.6

(Large deviation principle)
.

Let
\mathrm{I}
be a rate function on
\mathcal{E}
. The sequence
\{\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
is said to satisfy the
large deviation principle (LDP)
on
\mathcal{E}
with the rate function
\mathrm{I}
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

(Large deviation upper bound). For each closed subset
\mathrm{F}
of
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\ln\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{F})\leq-\mathrm{I}(\mathrm{F}).

(2)

(Large deviation lower bound). For each open subset
\mathrm{G}
of
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\ln\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{G})\geq-\mathrm{I}(\mathrm{G}).

2.3.
Controlled Poisson random measure

In rest of the paper, we use the following notations. Let
\mathrm{Z}
be a locally compact Polish space. Let
\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z})
be the space of all measures
\nu
on
(\mathrm{Z},\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}))
such that
\nu(K)<\infty
, for every compact subset
K
of
\mathrm{Z}
, and set
{\mathrm{C}}_{0}(\mathrm{Z})
as the space of continuous functions with compact supports. Endow the space
\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z})
with the weakest topology such that for every
f\in{\mathrm{C}}_{0}(\mathrm{Z})
, the function
\nu\mapsto\langle\nu,f\rangle=\int_{\mathrm{Z}}f(z)\mathrm{d}\nu(z),\;\nu\in\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z})
is continuous. Under the topology defined above, the space
\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z})
is a Polish space. For any fixed
T\in(0,\infty)
and let
\mathrm{Z}_{T}=[0,T]\times\mathrm{Z}
. Fix a measure
\nu\in\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z})
, and let
\nu_{T}=\lambda_{T}\otimes\nu
, where
\lambda_{T}
is Lebesgue measure on
[0,T]
.

Let us recall that a Poisson random measure
\boldsymbol{n}
on
\mathrm{Z}_{T}
with the intensity measure
\nu_{T}
is a
\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z}_{T})
-valued random variable such that for each
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}_{T})
with
\nu_{T}(B)<\infty,\;\boldsymbol{n}(B)
is a Poisson distribution with mean
\nu_{T}(B)
and for disjoint events
B_{1},\ldots,B_{j}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}_{T})
,
\boldsymbol{n}(B_{1}),\ldots,\boldsymbol{n}(B_{j})
are mutually independent random variables (see
[
35
]
). Denote by
\mathbb{P},
the measure induced by
\boldsymbol{n}
on
(\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z}_{T}),\mathcal{B}(\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z}_{T})))
. Then letting
\mathbb{M}=\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Z}_{T}),\;\mathbb{P}
is the unique probability measure on
(\mathbb{M},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{M}))
under the canonical map,
N:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{M},\;N(m)\doteq m
, is a Poisson random measure with the intensity measure
\nu_{T}
. With applications to large deviations, we consider for any
\theta>0,
probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
on
(\mathbb{M},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{M}))
under which
N
is a Poisson random measure with the intensity
\theta\nu_{T}
. The corresponding expectation operators will be denoted by
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
(for
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
), respectively.

For a metric space
\mathcal{E}
, the space of all real valued bounded
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})\backslash\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
-measurable maps and real-valued bounded continuous functions is denoted by
\mathrm{M}_{b}(\mathcal{E})
and
{\mathrm{C}}_{b}(\mathcal{E})
, respectively. Now, set
\mathrm{Y}=\mathrm{Z}\times[0,\infty)
and
\mathrm{Y}_{T}=[0,T]\times\mathrm{Y}
. Similarly,
\bar{\mathbb{M}}=\mathscr{M}_{FC}(\mathrm{Y}_{T})
and let
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
be the unique probability measure on
(\bar{\mathbb{M}},\mathcal{B}(\bar{\mathbb{M}}))
under which the canonical map
\bar{N}:\bar{\mathbb{M}}\to\bar{\mathbb{M}},\;\bar{N}(\bar{m})\doteq\bar{m}
, is a Poisson random measure with the intensity measure
\bar{\nu}_{T}=\lambda_{T}\otimes\nu\otimes\lambda_{\infty}
, where
\lambda_{\infty}
is the Lebesgue measure on
[0,\infty).
The corresponding expectation operator is denoted by
\bar{\mathbb{E}}
. Let
\mathscr{F}_{T}=\sigma\{\bar{N}((0,s)\times O):0\leq s\leq t,\;O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Y})\}
, and denote its completion by
\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{T}
under
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
. Set
\bar{\mathscr{P}}
as the predictable
\sigma
-field on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{M}
with the filtration
\{\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{t}:0\leq t\leq T\}
on
(\bar{\mathbb{M}},\mathcal{B}(\bar{\mathbb{M}}))
. Let
\bar{\mathcal{A}}
be the class of all
(\bar{\mathscr{P}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}))\backslash\mathcal{B}[0,\infty)
-measurable maps
\varphi:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\times\bar{\mathbb{M}}\to[0,\infty)
. For
\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}
, we suppress the argument
\bar{m}
in
\varphi(s,x,\bar{m})
and for simplicity, we write
\varphi(s,x)=\varphi(s,x,\bar{m})
. For
\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}
, define a counting process
N^{\varphi}
on
\mathrm{Z}_{T}
by

\displaystyle N^{\varphi}((0,T]\times U)=\int_{(0,T]\times U}\int_{(0,\infty)}\chi_{[0,\varphi(s,r)]}(r)\bar{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}r),\;t\in[0,T],\ U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}),

where
\chi_{A}
denotes the characteristic function of the set
A
. Note that
N^{\varphi}
is the controlled Poisson measure, with selecting intensity for the points at location
z
and time
s
in a possibly random but nonanticipating way. When
\varphi(s,z,\bar{m})\equiv\theta\in(0,\infty)
, we write
N^{\varphi}=N^{\theta}
. It should also be noted that
N^{\theta}
has the same distribution with respect to
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
as
N
has with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
.

2.4.
Poisson random measure and Wiener process

Set
\mathbf{W}={\mathrm{C}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{\infty}),\;\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{W}\times\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbf{\bar{V}}=\mathbf{W}\times\bar{\mathbb{M}}
. Then, define a mapping
N^{\mathbf{V}}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbb{M}
by
N^{\mathbf{V}}(w,m)=m
, for
(w,m)\in\mathbf{V}
, and define
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbf{V}}=\{\beta_{j}^{\mathbf{V}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
by
\beta_{j}^{\mathbf{V}}(w,m)=w_{j}
for
(w,m)\in\mathbf{V}
. Similarly, we can define the maps
\bar{N}^{\mathbf{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}}:\bar{\mathbf{V}}\to\bar{\mathbb{M}}
and
\mathrm{W}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}=\{\beta_{j}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\}
. Define the
\sigma
-filtration
\mathscr{H}_{t}^{\mathbf{V}}:=\sigma\{N^{\mathbf{V}}((0,s]\times O),\beta_{j}^{\mathbf{V}}(s):0\leq s\leq t,O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}),\;j\in\mathbb{N}\}
. For every
\theta>0,\;\mathbb{P}_{\theta}^{\mathbf{V}}
denotes the unique probability measure on
(\mathbf{V},\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{V}))
such that:

(1)

\{\beta_{j}^{\mathbf{V}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is an independent and identically distributed family of standard Brownian motions;

(2)

N^{\mathbf{V}}
is a Poisson random measure with the intensity measure
\theta\nu_{T}
.

Analogously, we can define
(\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}},\bar{\mathscr{H}}_{t}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}})
and denote
\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta=1}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}
by
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}
. We denote the
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}
-completion of
\{\bar{\mathscr{H}}_{t}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\}
by
\{\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{t}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\}
and the predictable
\sigma
-field
\bar{\mathscr{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}
on
[0,T]\times\bar{\mathbf{V}}
with the filtration
\{\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{t}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\}
on
(\bar{\mathbf{V}},\mathcal{B}(\bar{\mathbf{V}}))
. Let
{\mathcal{\bar{A}_{+}}}
be the class of all
(\bar{\mathcal{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}))\backslash\mathcal{B}[0,\infty)
-measurable maps
\varphi:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\times\bar{\mathbf{V}}\to[0,\infty)
. Define
\ell:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\ell(r)=r\ln r-r+1,\;r\in[0,\infty).

For any
\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{+}
the quantity

\displaystyle L_{T}(\varphi)=\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}\ell(\varphi(t,z,\omega))\nu_{T}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z),

is well defined as a
[0,\infty)
-valued random variable.
Denote
\mathcal{I}_{2},
the Hilbert space of all real sequences
\boldsymbol{x}=\{\boldsymbol{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{2}<\infty
, with the usual inner product. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\psi:\psi\text{ is }\bar{\mathcal{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\backslash\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\text{ measurable and }\int_{0}^{T}\|\psi(s)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,\;\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\text{-a.s.}}}\right\}.

Set
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{L}_{2}\times\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{+}
. Define

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{T}(\psi):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\psi(s)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s,\ \text{ for }\ \psi\in\mathcal{L}_{2},

and

(2.10)

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{T}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\widetilde{L}_{T}(\psi)+L_{T}(\varphi)\ \text{ for }\ \boldsymbol{q}=(\psi,\varphi)\in\mathcal{U}.

Let
\{K_{n}\subset\mathrm{Z},n=1,2,\dots\}
be an increasing family of compact sets such that
\bigcup\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{n}=\mathrm{Z}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b,n}\doteq\bigg\{\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{+}

\displaystyle:\text{ for all }(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\bar{\mathbb{M}},\;n\geq\varphi(t,z,\omega)\geq\frac{1}{n}\text{ if }z\in K_{n}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\varphi(t,z,\omega)=1\text{ if }z\in K_{n}^{c}\bigg\},

and let
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b}=\bigcup\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b,n}
. Considering
\varphi
as a control that perturbs jump rates away from 1, when
\varphi\neq 1
, we see that controls in
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b}
are bounded and perturb only off a compact set, where the bounds and set can depend on
\varphi
. The following representation formula is established in
[
19
]
(see Theorem 2.4,
[
18
]
also).

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
F\in\mathrm{M}_{b}(\mathbb{M})
. Then for any
\theta>0
,

\displaystyle-\ln\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\big[e^{-F(N)}\big]

\displaystyle=-\ln\bar{\mathbb{E}}\big[e^{-F(N^{\theta})}\big]=\inf_{\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{+}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\big[\theta L_{T}(\varphi)+F(N^{\theta\varphi})\big]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b}}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\big[\theta L_{T}(\varphi)+F(N^{\theta\varphi})\big].

2.5.
A general large deviation result

In this subsection, we recall a general criterion on the LDP established in
[
20
]
. Let
\{\mathscr{G^{\epsilon}}\}_{\epsilon>0}
be a family of measurable maps from
\bar{\mathbf{V}}
to
\mathbb{U}
, where
\mathbb{U}
is some Polish space. Let us present a sufficient condition for the LDP to hold for the family
\big\{\mathscr{G}^{\epsilon}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathrm{W},\epsilon N^{\epsilon^{-1}})\big\}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Let us define

\displaystyle S^{\Upsilon}=\big\{g:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\to[0,\infty):L_{T}(g)\leq\Upsilon\}

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}^{\Upsilon}=\big\{f:\mathrm{L}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{I}_{2}):\widetilde{L}_{T}(f)\leq\Upsilon\}.

A function
g\in S^{\Upsilon}
can be identified with a measure
\nu_{T}^{g}\in\mathbb{M}
defined by

\displaystyle\nu_{T}^{g}(O)=\int_{O}g(s,z)\nu_{T}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)\ \text{ for }\ O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}_{T}).

From Appendix A.1,
[
18
]
, we know that this identification induces a topology on
S^{\Upsilon}
under which
S^{\Upsilon}
is a compact space and in the sequel, we use this topology on
S^{\Upsilon}
. Set
\bar{S}^{\Upsilon}=\widetilde{S}^{\Upsilon}\times S^{\Upsilon}
. We define
\mathbb{S}=\bigcup\limits_{\Upsilon\geq 1}\bar{S}^{\Upsilon}
, and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\Upsilon}=\{\boldsymbol{q}=(\psi,\varphi)\in\mathcal{U}:\boldsymbol{q}(\omega)\in\bar{S}^{\Upsilon},\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{\bar{\mathbf{V}}}\text{-a.e. }\omega\}.

From
[
18
,
19
]
, we know that the following condition is sufficient for proving the LDP for a family
\big\{\mathscr{G}^{\epsilon}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathrm{W},\epsilon N^{\epsilon^{-1}})\big\}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Condition 2.1
.

There exists a measurable map
\mathscr{G}^{0}:\bar{\mathbf{V}}\to\mathbb{U}
such that the following hold:

(1)

For any
\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\boldsymbol{p}_{n}=(f_{n},g_{n}),\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\bar{S}^{\Upsilon}
be such that
\boldsymbol{p}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{p}
as
n\to\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}f_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\nu_{T}^{g_{n}}}}\right)\to\mathscr{G}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\nu_{T}^{g}}}\right)\ \text{ as }\ n\to\infty.

(2)

For any
\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\boldsymbol{q}_{\epsilon}=(\psi_{\epsilon},\varphi_{\epsilon}),\ \boldsymbol{q}=(\psi,\varphi)\in\mathcal{U}^{\Upsilon}
be such that
\boldsymbol{q}_{\epsilon}
converges in distribution to
\boldsymbol{q}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{\epsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathrm{W}(\cdot)+\int_{0}^{\cdot}\psi_{\epsilon}\mathrm{d}s,\epsilon N^{\epsilon^{-1}\varphi_{\epsilon}}}}\right)\Rightarrow\mathscr{G}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\psi(s)\mathrm{d}s,\nu_{T}^{\varphi}}}\right)\ \text{ as }\ \epsilon\to 0,

where
``\Rightarrow"
denotes the convergence in distribution.

It should be noted that

(1)

the condition (1) requires continuity in the control for deterministic controlled systems,

(2)

the condition (2) is a law of large numbers result for stochastic controlled systems with small noise.

In both cases, we are allowed to assume the controls take values in a compact set.

For
\phi\in\mathbb{U}
, define
\mathbb{S}_{\phi}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}:\phi=\mathscr{G}^{0}\big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\nu_{T}^{g}\big)}}\right\}
. Let
\mathrm{I}:\mathbb{U}\to[0,\infty]
be defined by

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(\phi)=\inf_{\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}_{\phi}}\bar{L}_{T}(\boldsymbol{p}),\ \text{ for }\ \phi\in\mathbb{U},

with the convention
\mathrm{I}(\phi)=\infty
if
\mathbb{S}_{\phi}=\varnothing
, where
\bar{L}_{T}(\cdot)
is defined in (
2.10
).

Let us recall an important result form
[
20
]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

For
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}
be defined by
\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}=\mathscr{G}^{\epsilon}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathrm{W},\epsilon N^{\epsilon^{-1}})
and suppose that Condition
2.1
holds true. Then
\mathrm{I}
defined in (
2.11
) is a rate function on
\mathbb{U}
and the family
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
satisfies the LDP with the rate function
\mathrm{I}
.

The following strengthened form of Theorem
2.8
is useful in applications (see Appendix A.2,
[
18
]
). Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\Upsilon}=\mathcal{U}^{\Upsilon}\cap\{\boldsymbol{q}=(\psi,\varphi):\varphi\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{b}\}
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose that Condition
2.1
holds true with
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\Upsilon}
instead of
\mathcal{U}^{\Upsilon}
. Then the conclusions of Theorem
2.8
continue to hold.

3.
LDP for the system (
1.1
)

Our goal in this work is to obtain a Wentzell-Freidlin type LDP for the solution to the system (
1.1
) as
\epsilon\to 0
on
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
, the space of càdlàg functions from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{H}
. Let
\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(\cdot)
be the unique solution to the system (
1.1
) with the initial condition
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
. In this section, we state the LDP on
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
for the solution
\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}(\cdot)
under appropriate assumptions.

Choose
\mathbb{U}=\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
in Condition
2.1
with the Skorokhod topology
\mathbb{U}_{S}
. We already know that
(\mathbb{U},\mathbb{U}_{S})
is a Polish space. For any
p>0
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{p}=\bigg\{

\displaystyle h:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}:\text{ there exists }\delta>0,\text{ such that for all }E\in\mathcal{B}([0,T])\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ with }\nu_{T}(E)<\infty,\text{ we have }\int_{E}\exp\big(\delta h^{p}(s,z)\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t<\infty\bigg\}.

It is easy to verify that
\mathcal{H}_{p}\subset\mathcal{H}_{q}
, for any
q\in(0,p)
and

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bigg\{h:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}:\sup_{(t,z)\in\mathrm{Z}_{T}}h(t,z)<\infty\bigg\}\subset\mathcal{H}_{p},\ \text{ for all }\ p>0.

In the sequel, we need the following lemmas. The proof of the following lemma can be obtained from Lemma 3.4,
[
18
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}(\nu_{T}),\;p^{\prime}\in(0,p]
, there exists a constant
C_{h,p,p^{\prime},\Upsilon}
such that

\displaystyle C_{h,p,p^{\prime},\Upsilon}:=\sup_{g\in S^{\Upsilon}}\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}h^{p^{\prime}}(s,z)(g(s,z)+1)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

For any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\nu_{T})
, there exists a constant
C_{h,\Upsilon}
such that

\displaystyle C_{h,\Upsilon}:=\sup_{g\in S^{\Upsilon}}\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}h(s,z)|g(s,z)-1|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

The proof of the following lemma can be obtained from Lemma 3.3,
[
77
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

(1)

If
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\mathbf{Y}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\infty
, for any
\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}(\cdot,\mathbf{Y}(\cdot))f(\cdot)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\;\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(\cdot,\mathbf{Y}(\cdot),z)(g(\cdot,z)-1)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

(2)

If the family of mappings
\{\mathbf{Y}_{n}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{H},\ n\in\mathbb{N}\}
satisfies
C=\sup\limits_{n}\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\|\mathbf{Y}_{n}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}^{\Upsilon}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\bar{S}^{\Upsilon}}\sup_{n}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(s,\mathbf{Y}_{n}(s),z)(g(s,z)-1)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{B}(s,\mathbf{Y}_{n}(s))f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}s\bigg]

\displaystyle<\infty.

The proof of which can be obtained from Lemma 3.11,
[
18
]
(see Lemma 2.8,
[
12
]
also).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
h:\mathrm{Z}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}|h(s,z)|^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s<\infty,

and for all
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and
K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Z}_{T})
satisfying
\nu_{T}(K)<\infty
,

\displaystyle\int_{K}\exp\big(\delta|h(s,z)|\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

(1)

Fix
\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
g_{n},g\in S^{\Upsilon}
be such that
g_{n}\to g
as
n\to\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}h(s,z)(g_{n}(s,z)-1)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}h(s,z)(g(s,z)-1)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s.

(2)

Fix
\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
\epsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathrm{Z}
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in S^{\Upsilon}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K_{\epsilon}^{c}}|h(s,z)||g(s,z)-1|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s\leq\epsilon.

(3)

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for any
K\subset\mathcal{B}([0,T])
satisfying
\lambda_{T}(K)<\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in S^{\Upsilon}}\int_{K}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}h(s,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s\leq\epsilon.

In order to prove LDP for the solution to the system (
1.1
), we need some additional assumptions on the noise coefficients stated below:

Hypothesis 3.4
.

The coefficients
\mathrm{B}
and
\gamma
satisfy the following assumptions:

(H.7)

There exists a function
L_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\mathrm{B}(t,\boldsymbol{y})\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq L_{\mathrm{B}}(t)\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},\ \text{ for all }\ (t,\boldsymbol{x}),(t,\boldsymbol{y})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{V}.

(H.8)

There exist positive constants
\eta_{0}
,
p\geq M
with
M:=\frac{2\alpha(\beta-1)(\beta+\eta_{0})}{\beta}\vee\frac{4(\beta-1)(\beta+\eta_{0})}{\beta}\vee 4\vee(\alpha+2)
and
L_{\gamma}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathrm{L}^{4}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\alpha+2}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathrm{L}^{M}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\frac{M}{2}}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathcal{H}_{p}
such that

\displaystyle\|\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x},z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq L_{\gamma}(t,z)(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}),\ \text{ for all }\ (t,\boldsymbol{x},z)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{V}\times\mathrm{Z}.

(H.9)

There exists a function
R_{\gamma}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\nu_{T})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\|\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{x},z)-\gamma(t,\boldsymbol{y},z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq R_{\gamma}(t,z)\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{\mathbb{H}},\ \text{ for all }\ (t,z)\in[0,T]\times\mathrm{Z},\;\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}.

Remark 3.5
.

The assumptions (H.7) and (H.9) are stronger than (H.5) and (H.6) (2) in Hypothesis
1.1
, respectively. The assumption (H.8) implies Hypothesis
1.1
(H.6) (3).

Let
\mathrm{I}:\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\to[0,\infty]
be defined as in (
2.11
). Our main result of this paper can be stated as follows:

Theorem 3.6
.

Assume that Hypotheses
1.1
and
3.4
hold. Then the family
\{\mathbf{Y}^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
satisfies the LDP on the space
\mathrm{D}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
with the rate function
\mathrm{I}(\cdot)
with respect to the topology of uniform convergence.

Proof.

The proof of this Theorem is straight forward from Theorem
2.8
. In view of Theorem
2.8
, it is sufficient to verify Condition
2.1
(1) and (2). Part (1) of Condition
2.1
is verified in Proposition
4.2
, and the remaining part (2) is established in Theorem
5.14
.
∎

4.
Verification of Condition
2.1
(1)

In this section, we verify Condition
2.1
(1), so that first part of the proof of Theorem
3.6
can be completed. We first consider a deterministic control problem for a class of partial differential equations with fully local monotone coefficients and discuss its solvability results using Lemmas
2.3
,
3.1
and
3.3
.

4.1.
Skeleton equations

We start by introducing the measurable map
\mathscr{G}^{0}
that is used in the definition of rate function
\mathrm{I}(\cdot)
given in (
2.11
) and also used for the verification of Condition
2.1
. Under appropriate assumptions, for every
\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}
, we show that the following deterministic control problem in
\mathbb{V}^{*}
:

(4.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)&=\bigg[\mathrm{A}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t))+\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t))f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t),z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\mathrm{d}t,\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H},\end{aligned}}}\right.

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
has a unique continuous weak solution.

We first prove the existence and uniqueness of weak solution of the deterministic control problem (
4.1
), using a Faedo-Galerkin approximation technique and exploiting the pseudo-monotonicity property of the nonlinear operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Assume
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}
. Under Hypotheses
1.1
and
3.4
, there exists a
unique weak solution

\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{A}(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{B}(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s))f(s)\mathrm{d}s

(4.2)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s),z)\big(g(s,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}s,\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V}^{*}.

Moreover, for fixed
\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a positive constant
C
such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{p}\in\bar{S}^{\Upsilon}}\bigg[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}}\right).

Proof.

The proof of this theorem is divided into the following steps:

Step 1:

An abstract operator.
First we define the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{*}
by

(4.4)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{C}(\cdot,\phi),\xi\rangle=-\langle\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\phi),\xi\rangle-\big(\mathrm{B}(\cdot,\phi)f(\cdot),\xi\big)-\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(\cdot,\phi,z)\big(g(\cdot,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\xi\bigg),

for all
\phi,\xi\in\mathbb{V}
. For simplicity of notations, in this step, we suppress the dependency of the first variable in each of the operators and functions.

The operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
is well-defined:
By the definition of the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
, we have for all
\xi\in\mathbb{V}

\displaystyle\big|\langle\mathfrak{C}(\phi),\xi\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\langle\mathrm{A}(\phi),\xi\rangle\big|+\big|\big(\mathrm{B}(\phi)f,\xi\big)\big|+\bigg|\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(\phi,z)\big(g(z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\xi\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{A}(\phi)\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}+\|\mathrm{B}(\phi)\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\gamma(\phi,z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big|g(z)-1\big|\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\nu(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\mathrm{A}(\phi)\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}+1\big)\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}}\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{C\big[\big(\mathfrak{a}+C\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\big)\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}\big)+1\big]+\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)

(4.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg\}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{V}},

where we have used Hypothesis
1.1
(H.4)-(H.6), Young’s and Hölder’s inequalities.
Therefore from (
4.1
), we conclude

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{C}(\phi)\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{C\big(\mathfrak{a}+C\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\big)\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}\big)+1+\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big(1+\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg\}.

Hence, we rewrite the integral equation (
4.1
) in the following form:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)=\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{C}(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(s))\mathrm{d}s,\ \text{ for all }\ t\in[0,T],

in the triplet
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{V}^{*}
. Let us establish some properties of the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
, which will help us to obtain the existence of a unique weak solution of the problem (
4.1
).

The operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
is coercive:
By using Hypotheses
1.1
(H.3), (H.5),
3.4
(H.8), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we have for all
\phi\in\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{C}(\phi),\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\geq L_{\mathrm{A}}\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}-\bigg[\mathfrak{a}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)

(4.8)

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\{\mathfrak{a}+2\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg\}\|\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\bigg],

which implies that the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
is coercive.

The operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
is fully locally monotone:
The following local monotnocity condition holds for the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{C}(\phi_{1})-\mathfrak{C}(\phi_{2}),\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathfrak{a}+\rho(\phi_{1})+\eta(\phi_{2})\big)+\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}R_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg\}\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

(4.9)

\displaystyle\quad\geq 0\ \text{ for all }\ \phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathbb{V}.

The above condition (
4.1
) can be verified as follows: From the definition of
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{C}(\phi_{1})-\mathfrak{C}(\phi_{2}),\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathrm{A}(\phi_{1})-\mathrm{A}(\phi_{2}),\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\rangle-\big((\mathrm{B}(\phi_{1})-\mathrm{B}(\phi_{2}))f,\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\big)

(4.10)

\displaystyle\quad-\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\big(\gamma(\phi_{1},z)-\gamma(\phi_{2},z)\big)\big(g(z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\bigg).

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities, we estimate the penultimate term in the right hand side of (
4.1
) as

\displaystyle\big|\big((\mathrm{B}(\phi_{1})-\mathrm{B}(\phi_{2}))f,\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathrm{B}(\phi_{1})-\mathrm{B}(\phi_{2}))f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\mathrm{B}(\phi_{1})-\mathrm{B}(\phi_{2})\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Making use of Hypothesis
3.4
(H.9), the Cauchy-Schwarz and Hölder’s inequalities for the final term in the right hand side of (
4.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\big(\gamma(\phi_{1},z)-\gamma(\phi_{2},z)\big)\big(g(z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\bigg)\bigg|

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}R_{\gamma}(z)\big|g(z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z).

Substituting (
4.1
) and (
4.1
) in (
4.1
), then using Hypothesis
1.1
(H.2), we deduce the required condition (
4.1
).

The operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
is hemicontinuous:
Let the operator
\mathfrak{B}(\cdot)
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{B}(\phi),\xi\rangle=-\big(\mathrm{B}(\phi)f(\cdot),\xi\big)-\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma(\phi,z)\big(g(\boldsymbol{z})-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\xi\bigg)\ \text{ for all }\ \phi,\xi\in\mathbb{V}.

Consider a sequence
\{\phi_{n}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{V}
such that
\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{\mathbb{V}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. For any
\xi\in\mathbb{V}
, we find

\displaystyle|\langle\mathfrak{B}(\phi_{n})-\mathfrak{B}(\phi),\xi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|((\mathrm{B}(\phi_{n})-\mathrm{B}(\phi))f,\xi)|+\bigg|\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\big(\gamma(\phi_{n},z)-\gamma(\phi,z)\big(g(z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\xi\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{B}(\phi_{n})-\mathrm{B}(\phi)\|_{\mathrm{L}_{2}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\|\gamma(\phi_{n},z)-\gamma(\phi,z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}|g(z)-1|\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\nu(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\mathrm{B}}\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{H}}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}R_{\gamma}(z)|g(z)-1|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)

(4.13)

\displaystyle\to 0\ \text{ as }n\to\infty,

where we have used Hypothesis
3.4
(H.7), (H.9). This implies the demiconitnuity of
\mathfrak{B}(\cdot)
and hence hemicontinuity also. Using the hemicontinuity property (see Hypothesis
1.1
(H.1)) of the operator
\mathrm{A}(\cdot)
, we conclude that the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)=-\mathrm{A}(\cdot)+\mathfrak{B}(\cdot)
is hemicontinuous.

Step 2:

Faedo-Galerkin approximation and finite dimensional problem.
Let us prove the existence of a weak solution to the equation (
4.1
) using a Faedo-Galerkin approximation, local monotonicity and hemicontinuity properties of the operator
\mathfrak{C}(\cdot)
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n},\ldots\}\subset\mathbb{V}
be a complete orthonormal system in
\mathbb{H}
and let
\mathbb{H}_{n}
be the
n
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{H}
spanned by
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\}
. Let us denote the projection of the space
\mathbb{V}^{*}
into
\mathbb{H}_{n}
by
\mathbf{P}_{n}
, that is,
\mathbf{P}_{n}\boldsymbol{x}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\rangle\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
. As we know that every element
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
induces a functional
\boldsymbol{x}^{*}\in\mathbb{H}
by the formula
\langle\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}),\;\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}
, then
\mathbf{P}_{n}\big|_{\mathbb{H}}
, the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathbb{H}_{n}
is given by
\mathbf{P}_{n}\boldsymbol{x}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{e}_{j})\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
. Clearly,
\mathbf{P}_{n}
is the orthogonal projection from
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathbb{H}_{n}
.

Define
\mathfrak{C}_{n}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot))=\mathbf{P}_{n}\mathfrak{C}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot))
. With the above setting, we consider the following system of ODEs:

(4.14)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\rangle&=-\langle\mathfrak{C}_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\rangle,\ \text{ for }\ t\in[0,T],\\
\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(0),\boldsymbol{\phi}\big)&=\big(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n},\boldsymbol{\phi}\big),\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n}=\mathbf{P}_{n}\boldsymbol{x}
for all
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
. By a classical result from Theorem 1.2.,
[
40
]
(cf. Theorem 3.3.1,
[
64
]
), there exists a unique local solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T^{*}];\mathbb{H}_{n})
, for some
0<T^{*}\leq T
. Let now show that
T=T^{*}
by establishing some uniform energy estimates.

Energy estimates:

Let us prove the uniform energy estimates satisfied by the system (
4.14
), which extend this local solution to the global one. Taking the inner product with
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot)
to the first equation of the system (
4.14
), we find

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathfrak{C}_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)\rangle\mathrm{d}s,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Using the coercivity condition (
4.1
) and Young’s inequality, we get for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2L_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\mathfrak{a}(s)+\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}(s)}\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(s,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+2\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\mathfrak{a}(s)+2\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}(s)}\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(s,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(2\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(s,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s

(4.15)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(s,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s,

An application of Gronwall’s inequality in (
4.1
) yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(2\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s\bigg\}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\bigg\{2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s\bigg\},

and the right hand side is finite for all
\boldsymbol{p}=(f,g)\in\mathbb{S}
(see Lemma
3.1
also). Substituting the above inequality (
4.1
) in (
4.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2L_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(2\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s\bigg\}

(4.17)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\bigg\{4\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\mathfrak{a}(s)+\mathfrak{b}(s)+\|f(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(s,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg)\mathrm{d}s\bigg\}.

Estimate for time derivative:
For
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
,
using the Cauchy-Schwarz, Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, and Hypotheses
1.1
(H.4), (H.5) and
3.4
(H.8), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle\big|\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\rangle\big|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)),\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\rangle\big|\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathrm{B}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))f(t),\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\big)\big|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg|\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t),z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\bigg)\bigg|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))\|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}}_{I_{1}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\underbrace{\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))f(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)}_{I_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\underbrace{\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}\|\gamma(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t),z)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big|g(t,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)}_{I_{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathfrak{a}(t)+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\big)\big(1+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}\big)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{\mathfrak{b}(t)}\big(1+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\|f(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(t,z)-1\big|\big(1+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}\bigg)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathfrak{a}(t)+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\big)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg(1+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathfrak{b}(t)+\|f(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)

(4.18)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(1+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathrm{Z}_{T}}L_{\gamma}(t,z)\big|g(t,z)-1\big|\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\mathrm{d}t\bigg)\bigg(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg).

In view of the energy estimate (
4.1
) and Lemma
3.1
, we find that the right hand of (
4.1
) is finite and independent of
n
, which implies that

(4.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*}),

for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
.

Step 3:

Existence of a weak solution.

Using the estimates (
4.1
) and (
4.1
), and an application of the Banach-Alaoglu theorem guarantee the existence of a subsequence
\{\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}\}
(still denoting by same index) such that

(4.20)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}&\xrightharpoonup{\ast}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}},&&\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}&\xrightharpoonup{}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}},&&\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),\;\beta\in(1,\infty)\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(T)&\xrightharpoonup{}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(T),&&\text{ in }\mathbb{H},\\
\mathrm{A}_{n}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot))&\xrightharpoonup{}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot),&&\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*}),\\
\mathrm{B}_{n}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot))f(\cdot)&\xrightharpoonup{}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(\cdot)f(\cdot),&&\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\\
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}&\xrightharpoonup{}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}},&&\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*}),\end{aligned}}}\right.

and

(4.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma_{n}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(\cdot))\big(g(\cdot,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z)\xrightharpoonup{}\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(\cdot,z)\big(g(\cdot,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

The convergences for
\mathrm{A}_{n},\mathrm{B}_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}
are justified by the estimates for
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
in (
4.1
). By an application of the Aubin-Lions compactness lemma, we conclude that

(4.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}},\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\ \text{ for }\ \beta\in(1,\infty).

Furthermore, along a subsequence (still denoting by same index), we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}},\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t\in[0,T]\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}.

By Proposition 23.23,
[
78
]
(see Theorem 1.8, pp. 33,
[
24
]
and Lemma 1.2, pp. 179,
[
73
]
also), we have the map
t\mapsto\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
is absolutely continuous with

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\rangle,\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t\in[0,T],

which is straightforward since
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
and
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*})
.

Now, our aim is to identify the limiting function obtained in the weak convergences (
4.20
) and (
4.21
).

Claim 1:
The functions
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p},\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot),\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(\cdot)
and
\widehat{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfy
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}\in\mathcal{M}:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V}):\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*})\}
and

(4.24)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)=\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t)+\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\ \text{ for a.e. }\ t\in(0,T),

also
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(0)=\boldsymbol{x}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)=\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)
.

The proof of Claim 1 for a similar model can be obtained from Lemma 30.5,
[
78
]
(cf. Lemma 2.4,
[
45
]
also). We include it here for completeness. We know that
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(\cdot)
satisfies the following integration by parts formula (Proposition 1.7.2,
[
25
]
):

(4.25)

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\boldsymbol{u}(T)\big)-\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(0),\boldsymbol{u}(0)\big)=\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t),\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t,

for
\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}\in\mathcal{M}
.

For any
\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}([0,T])
,
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
, from (
4.14
), we have

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(T),\psi(T)\boldsymbol{u}\big)-\big(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n},\psi(0)\boldsymbol{u}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\psi^{\prime}(t)\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t))+\mathrm{B}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t))f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t),z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{u}\bigg\rangle\mathrm{d}t

(4.26)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\psi^{\prime}(t)\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

Passing
n\to\infty
, we obtain for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\bigcup\limits_{n}\mathbb{H}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\psi(T)\boldsymbol{u}\big)-\big(\boldsymbol{x},\psi(0)\boldsymbol{u}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t)+\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{u}\bigg\rangle\mathrm{d}t

(4.27)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\psi^{\prime}(t)\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

We know that
\bigcup\limits_{n}\mathbb{H}_{n}
is dense in
\mathbb{V}
. Therefore (
4.1
) remains valid for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{V},\;\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}([0,T])
.

If we fix
\psi(T)=\psi(0)=0
, then we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t)+\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\boldsymbol{u}\bigg\rangle\psi(t)\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{T}\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\psi^{\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t,

and it gives (
4.24
) and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}\in\mathcal{M}
. Using integration by parts formula (
4.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\psi(T)\boldsymbol{u}\big)-\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(0),\psi(0)\boldsymbol{u}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{u}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\psi^{\prime}(t)\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

(4.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t)+\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{u}\bigg\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\psi^{\prime}(t)\boldsymbol{u},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t,

for all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{V},\;\psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}([0,T])
.
Using (
4.1
), we find

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\psi(T)\boldsymbol{u}\big)-\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(0),\psi(0)\boldsymbol{u}\big)=\big(\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\psi(T)\boldsymbol{u}\big)-\big(\boldsymbol{x},\psi(0)\boldsymbol{u}\big).

Then, by choosing
\psi(T)=1,\;\psi(0)=0
and
\psi(T)=0,\;\psi(0)=1
, respectively, we deduce

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)=\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T),\ \text{ and }\ \widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(0)=\boldsymbol{x},

hence the proof of Claim 1 is over.

Claim 2:

\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)=\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(\cdot))
, as an element in
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*})
, for
\beta\in(1,\infty)
.

Using the integration by parts formula, we get

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t))+\mathrm{B}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t))f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\gamma_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t),z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)\bigg\rangle\mathrm{d}t,

and

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t)+\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\bigg\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

Making use of the convergence (
4.20
) and the lower semicontinuity of
\mathbb{H}
-norm, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(T)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\big)-\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(t)f(t)+\int_{\mathrm{Z}}\widehat{\gamma}(t,z)\big(g(t,z)-1\big)\nu(\mathrm{d}z),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\bigg)\mathrm{d}t

(4.29)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

From the convergences (
4.20
) and (
4.1
), one can apply Lemma
2.3
to find for any
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathrm{A}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\langle\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(t),\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t.

Due to the arbitrary choice of
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{\beta}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, and uniqueness of the limit, we get
\mathrm{A}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(\cdot))=\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
as an element in the space
\mathrm{L}^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{*})
.

Claim 3:

\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(\cdot)f(\cdot)=\mathrm{B}(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(\cdot))f(\cdot)
and
\widehat{\gamma}(\cdot,z)\big(g(\cdot,z)-1\big)=\gamma(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{p}(\cdot),z)\big(g(\cdot,z)-1\big)
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
:

Our first aim is to prove the following:

(4.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|\big(\mathrm{B}_{n}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))-\mathrm{B}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\big)f(t)\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t=0.

For any
\delta>0
, define
A_{\delta,n}=\{t\in[0,T]:\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n^{\prime}}(t)-\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}>\delta\}
. Then by using the measure theoretic version of Markov’s inequality and the strong convergence (
4.22
), we have

(4.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{T}\big(A_{\delta,n}\big)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)-\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t}{\delta^{\beta}}=0.

Let us fix
K=\sup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\vee\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}<\infty
. We consider

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|(\mathbf{P}_{n}\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t))-\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)))f(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t

(4.32)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big\|\mathbf{P}_{n}\big(\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))-\mathrm{B}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\big)f(t)\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\big\|(\mathrm{I}-\mathbf{P}_{n})\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))f(t)\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t.

We consider the first term from the right hand side of the above inequality (
4.1
) and estimate it using Hypothesis
3.4
(H.7), (
4.31
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\|\mathbf{P}_{n}\big(\mathrm{B}(t,\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}(t))-\mathrm{B}(t,\mathbf{Y}^{\boldsymbol{p}}(t)\big)f(t)\big\|_{\mathbb{H}}\mathrm{d}t
